  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is... my body just won’t unclench. My shoulders and neck are tight all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends. I teach preschool, so there’s always some level of chaos, you know—tiny chairs, paint everywhere, someone needing help with a zipper—but this feels different.\n\nBy the time I get home, my jaw aches so much I can barely enjoy playing guitar, and that’s usually the thing that helps me unwind. So... yeah, that’s what pushed me to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tension in your body?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months now, I think. It started after I got fired from the preschool, and I kept thinking it would settle down once I got my bearings again, but it just sort of stayed. Funny, I still remember packing up all the little art projects and spare crayons—anyway, yeah, about fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overwhelmed, definitely. It’s like my mind won’t stay on one worry—it keeps jumping around. One minute I’m thinking, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then it’s, “What if the parents from before think I’m unsafe or incompetent?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I loved working with those kids.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about retirement, like I’ve somehow pushed it farther away forever. Or I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like if I did a story-time activity wrong or missed something small, and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” It sounds extreme when I say it, but in my head it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started experiencing the bodily tension?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time. I mean… I get discouraged, and there are days where everything feels heavy, but “sad” is a tricky word. I think more often I feel keyed up, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, with preschoolers, sadness is so obvious sometimes—like when a child’s block tower falls over and it’s the end of the world for two minutes, then they’re fine after snack. Adults are messier, I guess. So… no, I don’t know if I’d call it being down most of the time. More like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up, but “reached a peak within minutes”… I don’t know. That makes me think of something very sudden, like a fire drill going off in a classroom and everyone jumps at once.\n\nMine feels more… I don’t know, more like it builds while I’m thinking about things. But I may not be understanding exactly what you mean by an episode.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually anxious about a specific situation or a wide range of everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one specific situation, no. I wish it were, honestly, because then maybe I could avoid it or make a plan for it.\n\nIt kind of jumps all over. I’ll be worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then suddenly I’m thinking, well, what if I’ve ruined my reputation with parents? You know, parents talk—at least they did at school pickup, though half the time it was about lunch containers or who had a runny nose—but still, I start imagining they all think I’m unsafe or incompetent.\n\nThen it’ll veer into retirement, like I’ve somehow set myself back permanently. And then, out of nowhere, I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like a story-time activity where maybe I didn’t handle it perfectly, and my mind turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which… saying it out loud, I know that sounds like a huge leap. But in the moment it feels real. So yes, it’s a pretty wide range of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it comes out of nowhere. That phrase makes me picture, you know, you’re washing a mug at the sink and suddenly your whole body thinks there’s a bear in the kitchen.\n\nFor me, when I get scared or uncomfortable, there’s usually some thread I can follow—some thought or worry I was already chewing on. Though sometimes the thread is so tangled I don’t notice it right away. It’s like when a child starts crying and you think it’s random, but then you find out someone took the blue crayon ten minutes ago and they’ve been holding it in the whole time.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed a sudden “out of nowhere” kind of episode, at least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble pinning that down. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds so specific, and I don’t really clock it that way when it happens. I’m usually already caught in a worry, and then it sort of… swells, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s minutes or longer.\n\nIt’s not like I’m timing it. I mean, in a classroom we used timers for everything—cleanup, handwashing, lining up—and somehow with my own feelings I have no timer at all. So I can’t honestly say, yes, it peaks in a few minutes. I just know I get really distressed sometimes, but the timing is fuzzy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to pass out when you were feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I’ve worried myself into feeling really awful, but “heart attack” or “I’m going to pass out” — I don’t know, that sounds more dramatic and specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m anxious, my mind goes straight to consequences, if that makes sense, not so much my body. Like, I’ll be thinking, “What if this ruins everything?” rather than “Oh no, my heart.” Though then I start wondering if maybe I’m not paying attention properly, because in the classroom I used to notice every little change in a child’s face — if they were about to cry, or throw up, or need the bathroom — but with myself I can be strangely oblivious.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had that clear feeling of having a heart attack or definitely about to pass out. At least not that I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear that seemed to come on quickly, without any clear reason or warning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t recognized it that cleanly. When you say “without any clear reason or warning,” I sort of get stuck there, because usually I can trace it back to something, even if it’s a tiny thing. Like a parent email, or a schedule change, or just some thought that’s been buzzing around.\n\nIt’s funny, with kids you can see a meltdown building sometimes before they can—like they insist they’re fine, but you know the snack was late and their shoes feel wrong and the tower fell over, so of course it’s coming. With me, I’m not always that good at noticing the buildup.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve had that sudden bolt of intense fear for no reason. It feels more like there’s usually a reason somewhere, even if I don’t see it right away.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that you find yourself worrying about most often?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I guess—well, work and what work means, if that makes sense. My mind keeps going around and around about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then it branches off into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then I’ll think about retirement, like, “Have I messed that up too?” Which is ridiculous maybe, but it doesn’t feel ridiculous at 2 in the morning.\n\nEven little things get tied into it. Like I’ll remember some small mistake from a story-time activity—maybe I used the wrong voice for a character, or skipped a page, or didn’t handle a child’s question quite right—and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but it just keeps circling back there.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually focused on one specific thing or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts, I think. It’s not just one neat thing, even though work is kind of the center of it.\n\nMy mind will start with, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then somehow it’s about whether parents think badly of me, and then I’m worrying about retirement and whether I’ve let that slip away too. And then—this is the part that feels embarrassing—I’ll remember some tiny story-time mistake, like maybe I handled a child’s question awkwardly, and suddenly I’m thinking, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.”\n\nIt all feels connected, but it jumps around a lot. Like one worry opens the door to five more.\n\nDoctor: Is there one main thing that you worry about most of the time, or are there many different things that you worry about equally?\n\nPatient: It’s many things, but they all sort of tie back together, if that makes sense. Work is probably the biggest knot in the middle of it.\n\nI’ll start worrying, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then it turns into, “Have I ruined how parents see me?” And then somehow I’m thinking about retirement, like I’ve let that slip away too. Which—sorry, this reminds me of how I used to tell the kids, “One block at a time,” when they’d get overwhelmed building something, but my brain does the opposite. It knocks the whole tower down at once.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something tiny from story-time, like maybe I made a mistake or handled a question badly, and my mind turns it into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” So it’s not equal exactly, but it spreads. One worry becomes all the others.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or can it happen at any time?\n\nPatient: It can happen kind of anytime, but there are certain things that set it off more. Anything about work, especially—like seeing something about a school hiring, or thinking about whether I’ll ever get hired again.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay there. My mind keeps circling from that to, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then somehow I’m worrying about retirement, like it’s slipping away from me. And then I’ll remember some tiny thing from a story-time activity—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s like those memories have hooks in them—and I’ll think, “What if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore?”\n\nSo… yes, specific things can trigger it, but honestly it doesn’t need much. It can just start and then it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things that most people would not worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, they feel important to me in the moment, but when I say them out loud, they sound maybe bigger than they should be.\n\nLike my mind keeps circling from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether parents think I’ve ruined my reputation, and then somehow I’m thinking about retirement slipping away. And then I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity—something most people would probably forget by lunch—and I’m thinking, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?”\n\nSo yes, I guess I do worry about everyday things more than other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Is there one main worry that you keep coming back to, or are there many different worries that feel equally important to you?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve sort of said this already, but it’s not just one thing. Work is the one I keep landing on—whether I’ll ever get hired again—but then it spreads out from there.\n\nI’ll start with that and suddenly I’m thinking, have I ruined my reputation with parents? And then it jumps to retirement, like I’ve let that slip away too. And then, this is the part that feels almost silly when I say it, I’ll remember some tiny thing from story-time, like maybe I made the wrong call with a child’s question, and my mind turns it into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.”\n\nSo… I guess there are many worries, but they’re all connected somehow. One starts, and then the others line up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find yourself worrying about most things in your daily life or is it mainly focused on specific situations like work?\n\nPatient: It starts with work a lot, yes—like whether I’ll ever get hired again—but then it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. My mind keeps circling from that to whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, and then I’m suddenly thinking about retirement, like I’ve somehow let my whole future slip away.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something tiny, like from a story-time activity, maybe a small mistake or something I said oddly, and it turns into, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore?” Which… I know sounds extreme when I say it out loud.\n\nSo I guess it’s focused on work, but it spreads into almost everything connected to it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally made me come in is... my body just won’t unclench. My shoulders and neck are tight all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends. I teach preschool, so there’s always some level of chaos, you know—tiny chairs, paint everywhere, someone needing help with a zipper—but this feels different.\n\nBy the time I get home, my jaw aches so much I can barely enjoy playing guitar, and that’s usually the thing that helps me unwind. So... yeah, that’s what pushed me to make the appointment.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this tension in your body?\n\nPatient: About… a year and two months now, I think. It started after I got fired from the preschool, and I kept thinking it would settle down once I got my bearings again, but it just sort of stayed. Funny, I still remember packing up all the little art projects and spare crayons—anyway, yeah, about fourteen months.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed during this time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Overwhelmed, definitely. It’s like my mind won’t stay on one worry—it keeps jumping around. One minute I’m thinking, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then it’s, “What if the parents from before think I’m unsafe or incompetent?” Which is awful to even say out loud, because I loved working with those kids.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about retirement, like I’ve somehow pushed it farther away forever. Or I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like if I did a story-time activity wrong or missed something small, and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” It sounds extreme when I say it, but in my head it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started experiencing the bodily tension?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time. I mean… I get discouraged, and there are days where everything feels heavy, but “sad” is a tricky word. I think more often I feel keyed up, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, with preschoolers, sadness is so obvious sometimes—like when a child’s block tower falls over and it’s the end of the world for two minutes, then they’re fine after snack. Adults are messier, I guess. So… no, I don’t know if I’d call it being down most of the time. More like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up, but “reached a peak within minutes”… I don’t know. That makes me think of something very sudden, like a fire drill going off in a classroom and everyone jumps at once.\n\nMine feels more… I don’t know, more like it builds while I’m thinking about things. I wish it were, honestly, because then maybe I could avoid it or make a plan for it.\n\nIt kind of jumps all over. I’ll be worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then suddenly I’m thinking, well, what if I’ve ruined my reputation with parents? You know, parents talk—at least they did at school pickup, though half the time it was about lunch containers or who had a runny nose—but still, I start imagining they all think I’m unsafe or incompetent.\n\nThen it’ll veer into retirement, like I’ve somehow set myself back permanently. And then, out of nowhere, I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like a story-time activity where maybe I didn’t handle it perfectly, and my mind turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which… saying it out loud, I know that sounds like a huge leap. But in the moment it feels real. So yes, it’s a pretty wide range of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it comes out of nowhere. That phrase makes me picture, you know, you’re washing a mug at the sink and suddenly your whole body thinks there’s a bear in the kitchen.\n\nFor me, when I get scared or uncomfortable, there’s usually some thread I can follow—some thought or worry I was already chewing on. Though sometimes the thread is so tangled I don’t notice it right away. It’s like when a child starts crying and you think it’s random, but then you find out someone took the blue crayon ten minutes ago and they’ve been holding it in the whole time.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed a sudden “out of nowhere” kind of episode, at least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble pinning that down. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds so specific, and I don’t really clock it that way when it happens. I’m usually already caught in a worry, and then it sort of… swells, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s minutes or longer.\n\nIt’s not like I’m timing it. I mean, in a classroom we used timers for everything—cleanup, handwashing, lining up—and somehow with my own feelings I have no timer at all. So I can’t honestly say, yes, it peaks in a few minutes. I just know I get really distressed sometimes, but the timing is fuzzy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to pass out when you were feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I’ve worried myself into feeling really awful, but “heart attack” or “I’m going to pass out” — I don’t know, that sounds more dramatic and specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m anxious, my mind goes straight to consequences, if that makes sense, not so much my body. Like, I’ll be thinking, “What if this ruins everything?” rather than “Oh no, my heart.” Though then I start wondering if maybe I’m not paying attention properly, because in the classroom I used to notice every little change in a child’s face — if they were about to cry, or throw up, or need the bathroom — but with myself I can be strangely oblivious.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had that clear feeling of having a heart attack or definitely about to pass out. At least not that I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear that seemed to come on quickly, without any clear reason or warning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t recognized it that cleanly. When you say “without any clear reason or warning,” I sort of get stuck there, because usually I can trace it back to something, even if it’s a tiny thing. Like a parent email, or a schedule change, or just some thought that’s been buzzing around.\n\nIt’s funny, with kids you can see a meltdown building sometimes before they can—like they insist they’re fine, but you know the snack was late and their shoes feel wrong and the tower fell over, so of course it’s coming. With me, I’m not always that good at noticing the buildup.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve had that sudden bolt of intense fear for no reason. It feels more like there’s usually a reason somewhere, even if I don’t see it right away.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that you find yourself worrying about most often?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I guess—well, work and what work means, if that makes sense. My mind keeps going around and around about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then it branches off into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then I’ll think about retirement, like, “Have I messed that up too?” Which is ridiculous maybe, but it doesn’t feel ridiculous at 2 in the morning.\n\nEven little things get tied into it. Like I’ll remember some small mistake from a story-time activity—maybe I used the wrong voice for a character, or skipped a page, or didn’t handle a child’s question quite right—and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but it just keeps circling back there.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually focused on one specific thing or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts, I think. It’s not just one neat thing, even though work is kind of the center of it.\n\nMy mind will start with, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then somehow it’s about whether parents think badly of me, and then I’m worrying about retirement and whether I’ve let that slip away too. And then—this is the part that feels embarrassing—I’ll remember some tiny story-time mistake, like maybe I handled a child’s question awkwardly, and suddenly I’m thinking, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.”\n\nIt all feels connected, but it jumps around a lot. Like one worry opens the door to five more.\n\nDoctor: Is there one main thing that you worry about most of the time, or are there many different things that you worry about equally?\n\nPatient: It’s many things, but they all sort of tie back together, if that makes sense. Work is probably the biggest knot in the middle of it.\n\nI’ll start worrying, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then it turns into, “Have I ruined how parents see me?” And then somehow I’m thinking about retirement, like I’ve let that slip away too. Which—sorry, this reminds me of how I used to tell the kids, “One block at a time,” when they’d get overwhelmed building something, but my brain does the opposite. It knocks the whole tower down at once.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something tiny from story-time, like maybe I made a mistake or handled a question badly, and my mind turns it into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” So it’s not equal exactly, but it spreads. One worry becomes all the others.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or can it happen at any time?\n\nPatient: It can happen kind of anytime, but there are certain things that set it off more. Anything about work, especially—like seeing something about a school hiring, or thinking about whether I’ll ever get hired again.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay there. My mind keeps circling from that to, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then somehow I’m worrying about retirement, like it’s slipping away from me. And then I’ll remember some tiny thing from a story-time activity—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s like those memories have hooks in them—and I’ll think, “What if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore?”\n\nSo… yes, specific things can trigger it, but honestly it doesn’t need much. It can just start and then it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things that most people would not worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Or I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like if I did a story-time activity wrong or missed something small, and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” It sounds extreme when I say it, but in my head it feels very convincing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time since you started experiencing the bodily tension?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say sad most of the time. I mean… I get discouraged, and there are days where everything feels heavy, but “sad” is a tricky word. I think more often I feel keyed up, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong.\n\nIt’s funny, with preschoolers, sadness is so obvious sometimes—like when a child’s block tower falls over and it’s the end of the world for two minutes, then they’re fine after snack. Adults are messier, I guess. So… no, I don’t know if I’d call it being down most of the time. More like I can’t settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had moments where I get really worked up, but “reached a peak within minutes”… I don’t know. That makes me think of something very sudden, like a fire drill going off in a classroom and everyone jumps at once.\n\nMine feels more… I don’t know, more like it builds while I’m thinking about things. But I may not be understanding exactly what you mean by an episode.\n\nDoctor: Are you usually anxious about a specific situation or a wide range of everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s not just one specific situation, no. I wish it were, honestly, because then maybe I could avoid it or make a plan for it.\n\nIt kind of jumps all over. I’ll be worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then suddenly I’m thinking, well, what if I’ve ruined my reputation with parents? You know, parents talk—at least they did at school pickup, though half the time it was about lunch containers or who had a runny nose—but still, I start imagining they all think I’m unsafe or incompetent.\n\nThen it’ll veer into retirement, like I’ve somehow set myself back permanently. And then, out of nowhere, I’ll remember some tiny thing from class, like a story-time activity where maybe I didn’t handle it perfectly, and my mind turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which… saying it out loud, I know that sounds like a huge leap. But in the moment it feels real. So yes, it’s a pretty wide range of things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden episode of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come out of nowhere?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d say it comes out of nowhere. That phrase makes me picture, you know, you’re washing a mug at the sink and suddenly your whole body thinks there’s a bear in the kitchen.\n\nFor me, when I get scared or uncomfortable, there’s usually some thread I can follow—some thought or worry I was already chewing on. Though sometimes the thread is so tangled I don’t notice it right away. It’s like when a child starts crying and you think it’s random, but then you find out someone took the blue crayon ten minutes ago and they’ve been holding it in the whole time.\n\nSo… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed a sudden “out of nowhere” kind of episode, at least not in the way you’re describing.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to peak within a few minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m having trouble pinning that down. “Peak within a few minutes” sounds so specific, and I don’t really clock it that way when it happens. I’m usually already caught in a worry, and then it sort of… swells, I guess, but I don’t know if that’s minutes or longer.\n\nIt’s not like I’m timing it. I mean, in a classroom we used timers for everything—cleanup, handwashing, lining up—and somehow with my own feelings I have no timer at all. So I can’t honestly say, yes, it peaks in a few minutes. I just know I get really distressed sometimes, but the timing is fuzzy.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to pass out when you were feeling anxious?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way. I mean, I’ve worried myself into feeling really awful, but “heart attack” or “I’m going to pass out” — I don’t know, that sounds more dramatic and specific than what I’ve noticed.\n\nSometimes when I’m anxious, my mind goes straight to consequences, if that makes sense, not so much my body. Like, I’ll be thinking, “What if this ruins everything?” rather than “Oh no, my heart.” Though then I start wondering if maybe I’m not paying attention properly, because in the classroom I used to notice every little change in a child’s face — if they were about to cry, or throw up, or need the bathroom — but with myself I can be strangely oblivious.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve had that clear feeling of having a heart attack or definitely about to pass out. At least not that I can really say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear that seemed to come on quickly, without any clear reason or warning?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I haven’t recognized it that cleanly. When you say “without any clear reason or warning,” I sort of get stuck there, because usually I can trace it back to something, even if it’s a tiny thing. Like a parent email, or a schedule change, or just some thought that’s been buzzing around.\n\nIt’s funny, with kids you can see a meltdown building sometimes before they can—like they insist they’re fine, but you know the snack was late and their shoes feel wrong and the tower fell over, so of course it’s coming. With me, I’m not always that good at noticing the buildup.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t say I’ve had that sudden bolt of intense fear for no reason. It feels more like there’s usually a reason somewhere, even if I don’t see it right away.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or thing that you find yourself worrying about most often?\n\nPatient: Mostly work, I guess—well, work and what work means, if that makes sense. My mind keeps going around and around about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then it branches off into whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents.\n\nAnd then I’ll think about retirement, like, “Have I messed that up too?” Which is ridiculous maybe, but it doesn’t feel ridiculous at 2 in the morning.\n\nEven little things get tied into it. Like I’ll remember some small mistake from a story-time activity—maybe I used the wrong voice for a character, or skipped a page, or didn’t handle a child’s question quite right—and suddenly my brain turns that into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore.” And I know that sounds extreme when I say it out loud, but it just keeps circling back there.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually focused on one specific thing or does it seem to shift between many different things?\n\nPatient: It shifts, I think. It’s not just one neat thing, even though work is kind of the center of it.\n\nMy mind will start with, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then somehow it’s about whether parents think badly of me, and then I’m worrying about retirement and whether I’ve let that slip away too. And then—this is the part that feels embarrassing—I’ll remember some tiny story-time mistake, like maybe I handled a child’s question awkwardly, and suddenly I’m thinking, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.”\n\nIt all feels connected, but it jumps around a lot. Like one worry opens the door to five more.\n\nDoctor: Is there one main thing that you worry about most of the time, or are there many different things that you worry about equally?\n\nPatient: It’s many things, but they all sort of tie back together, if that makes sense. Work is probably the biggest knot in the middle of it.\n\nI’ll start worrying, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and then it turns into, “Have I ruined how parents see me?” And then somehow I’m thinking about retirement, like I’ve let that slip away too. Which—sorry, this reminds me of how I used to tell the kids, “One block at a time,” when they’d get overwhelmed building something, but my brain does the opposite. It knocks the whole tower down at once.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something tiny from story-time, like maybe I made a mistake or handled a question badly, and my mind turns it into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.” So it’s not equal exactly, but it spreads. One worry becomes all the others.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific that usually triggers your anxiety, or can it happen at any time?\n\nPatient: It can happen kind of anytime, but there are certain things that set it off more. Anything about work, especially—like seeing something about a school hiring, or thinking about whether I’ll ever get hired again.\n\nBut then it doesn’t stay there. My mind keeps circling from that to, “Have I ruined my reputation with parents?” and then somehow I’m worrying about retirement, like it’s slipping away from me. And then I’ll remember some tiny thing from a story-time activity—sorry, I know I keep coming back to that, but it’s like those memories have hooks in them—and I’ll think, “What if that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore?”\n\nSo… yes, specific things can trigger it, but honestly it doesn’t need much. It can just start and then it keeps going.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually worry about everyday things that most people would not worry about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, they feel important to me in the moment, but when I say them out loud, they sound maybe bigger than they should be.\n\nLike my mind keeps circling from whether I’ll ever get hired again, to whether parents think I’ve ruined my reputation, and then somehow I’m thinking about retirement slipping away. And then I’ll remember some tiny mistake from a story-time activity—something most people would probably forget by lunch—and I’m thinking, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?”\n\nSo yes, I guess I do worry about everyday things more than other people seem to.\n\nDoctor: Is there one main worry that you keep coming back to, or are there many different worries that feel equally important to you?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve sort of said this already, but it’s not just one thing. Work is the one I keep landing on—whether I’ll ever get hired again—but then it spreads out from there.\n\nI’ll start with that and suddenly I’m thinking, have I ruined my reputation with parents? And then it jumps to retirement, like I’ve let that slip away too. And then, this is the part that feels almost silly when I say it, I’ll remember some tiny thing from story-time, like maybe I made the wrong call with a child’s question, and my mind turns it into, “Maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children.”\n\nSo… I guess there are many worries, but they’re all connected somehow. One starts, and then the others line up behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually find yourself worrying about most things in your daily life or is it mainly focused on specific situations like work?\n\nPatient: It starts with work a lot, yes—like whether I’ll ever get hired again—but then it doesn’t stay neatly in that box. My mind keeps circling from that to whether I’ve ruined my reputation with parents, and then I’m suddenly thinking about retirement, like I’ve somehow let my whole future slip away.\n\nAnd then I’ll remember something tiny, like from a story-time activity, maybe a small mistake or something I said oddly, and it turns into, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children anymore?” Which… I know sounds extreme when I say it out loud.\n\nSo I guess it’s focused on work, but it spreads into almost everything connected to it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a wide range of everyday things or is it mostly focused on specific situations like your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work, I suppose, but it doesn’t stay just “work,” if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll start with, “Will I ever get hired again?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about whether parents think badly of me, whether I’ve ruined my reputation somehow. And then it jumps ahead to retirement, like I’ve let my whole future get away from me.\n\nEven little things get pulled into it. Like I’ll remember a story-time activity and think, maybe I handled some tiny moment wrong, and then my mind goes, “Well, maybe that means you shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which… sounds awful when I say it like that. So yes, it’s specific in one way, but it spreads into a lot of everyday things around it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about a specific situation or a wide range of everyday things?\n\nPatient: It’s… I’d say it starts with a specific thing, mostly work, but then it sort of blooms into everything else.\n\nLike I’ll think, “Am I ever going to get hired again?” and before I know it I’m thinking parents must think badly of me, or I’ve ruined my reputation somehow. Then it jumps to retirement, like I’ve let that whole part of my future slip away.\n\nAnd then I get stuck on these tiny moments, like something from story-time—some little thing I said or did—and suddenly my mind is treating it like proof that maybe I shouldn’t be trusted with children. Which I know sounds like a big leap, but that’s where it goes.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Mostly work, yes. Or… the teaching work, being trusted as a teacher, that whole thing. But once it starts there, it doesn’t stay neat.
